id: c-ap10-embodiment sortkey: 2010 title: AP10 · Embodiment & sensorimotor grounding — the "a mind needs a body" bet domains: [frontier, approaches-to-agi] level: core prereqs: [c-next-word, c-scaling-laws, c-ap1-scale, c-ap5-world-models] provides: [embodiment-the-bet, symbol-grounding-problem, moravecs-paradox, world-is-its-own-best-model, robot-foundation-model-vla, robot-data-bottleneck, grounding-vs-embodiment] resources: [] status: ready reading_time: 34 min rev: 1 created: 2026-07-16 updated: 2026-07-16

AP10 · Embodiment & sensorimotor grounding — the "a mind needs a body" bet

This is the ninth big idea we look at for how to build a machine that can think in a general way. Every bet so far has tried to build a mind out of information alone — words, pictures, video, reward numbers. This one says that is the deep mistake. A real mind, it argues, is not a program floating in a box; it is something that grew up acting on the physical world — moving, touching, bumping into things, and feeling what happens next. On this view, meaning itself comes from the body: you only truly know what "heavy" or "hot" or "in front of" means because you have lifted, been burned, and reached. So the road to real intelligence is not more text; it is to give the machine a body — eyes, hands, and the chance to act — and let it learn the world the way a child or an animal does, by doing. The idea has deep roots in philosophy and old robotics, and it has suddenly become the hottest area in applied AI, as the first "robot brains" trained like language models start to walk into warehouses and homes. This page explains it all from zero: the bet in one minute, the old and stubborn puzzle it rests on (the symbol grounding problem), what "embodiment" really claims, the new robots that are testing it, and the four places it is stuck.

You are here: this is the AP10 page — the tenth bet on the "approaches to AGI" map (see APPROACHES_TO_AGI), and the ninth approach card written in full. AGI means artificial general intelligence — a machine that can think across many different problems, not just one. The short name for this bet is embodiment (having a physical body that senses and acts), and its key claim is sensorimotor grounding — sensori for the senses (seeing, touching), motor for movement (reaching, walking); grounding means tying the machine's words and thoughts to real things in the world through those senses and movements.

This page builds on rungs of the ladder you have already climbed, all short and plain: guessing the next word — how today's AI learns from text; scaling laws & the data wall — the steady curve scale rides, and where the text runs out; AP1 · the "make it bigger" bet — the pure-text bet this one says has no body; and above all AP5 · world models / JEPA — its closest partner, where you met the idea that text is the wrong food and that most of what there is to know is watched, not written. A one-line reminder of each is given where it is used, so you will not get lost. AP10 is the deep home for the embodiment idea and the symbol grounding problem. (Its neighbour just below it on the map, AP9 · open-endedness, reads right before this page.)

Where the facts come from: the durable ideas are grounded in the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy entry on the Chinese Room (which lays out the "Robot Reply" and the symbol grounding problem), Stevan Harnad (who named that problem), Hans Moravec (Moravec's paradox), and Rodney Brooks's robotics work (quoted through Brian Christian's book The Alignment Problem). The new robots are grounded in the RT-2, PaLM-E, OpenVLA, and π0 papers (all in the corpus). A fresh check of the field, done on the web (as of July 2026), covers the newest robot brains and the humanoid-robot wave. Each fast-moving number is dated below.

The bet in one minute

Here is the whole idea, as short as it goes.

Today's leading AI learned everything it knows from human-made data — mostly text, plus some images and video. This bet says that is like trying to learn to swim by reading every book ever written about water: you can memorise a perfect description and still sink, because you have never actually been in it. On this view, real understanding is not information you are told; it is something you earn by acting. You know what "heavy" means because your arm has strained; you know "cup" not as a word linked to other words but as a thing you have grasped, tipped, and drunk from. A mind, the bet says, is built out of this loop of doing-and-feeling, and a machine that has never acted on the world is missing the very thing that gives its words meaning. So the path to general intelligence is to put the AI in a body — real or realistic — with senses and the power to move, and let it learn the world by living in it, the way every real mind we know of did. The champions of this bet are the roboticists and the "physical AI" labs now racing to build exactly that.

Why believe a mind needs a body? Because of one old and awkward question that pure-text AI has never really answered: how does a symbol get its meaning in the first place? A word in a language model is defined only by other words — "cat" sits near "dog" and "pet" and "fur." But those are just more words; the chain of words-explaining-words never touches an actual cat. It is like looking up a word in a dictionary written in a language you do not speak: every definition sends you to more words you also do not know. The embodiment bet says the chain has to end somewhere in the real world — in seeing a cat, hearing it, stroking it — and only a body can reach that far. Without it, the machine is shuffling symbols it does not truly understand.

That is the bet. The rest of this page explains the symbol grounding problem (why words-about-words may not be enough), what "embodiment" really claims, the new robots testing it, why serious people back it, and why it might still be wrong.

First, a one-line reminder of the base

Three quick reminders from rungs you have already read, so this page stands on its own.

  • From guessing the next word and AP1: today's main AI is a neural network trained to guess the next word in human text. A neural network is a huge web of simple number-units whose connection strengths are slowly tuned to fit data. It has read almost the whole internet — but it has never done anything; it only ever predicted symbols. AP10's whole complaint is about that "never done anything." (New to you? Read those two rungs first; they are short.)
  • From scaling laws & the data wall: making the machine bigger and feeding it more text makes its score climb in a steady line — but good human text is running out (the data wall). AP10 offers a way past that wall from a new direction: the world itself is an endless source of fresh experience, if a machine can only go out and act in it.
  • From AP5, the world-models bet: you already met its closest cousin. AP5 (Yann LeCun's bet) says text is the wrong food and a machine should instead watch the world (mostly video) to build an inner model of how it works. Two ideas from there come straight back: the sensory-bandwidth argument — a four-year-old's eyes take in far more information than all the text on the internet — and the point that most of what there is to know is watched, not written. Hold both; AP10 takes them one step further.

Now the one new idea this page adds. AP5 says: don't just read about the world — watch it. AP10 says that is still not enough, because watching is passive. To really grasp the world you must act on it and feel the result — push the cup and watch it slide, and learn from having caused it. That extra step, from watching to doing, is the whole of AP10. Its claim is that meaning is sensorimotor — born from the tight loop of moving your body and sensing what changes — and that a mind with no body, however much it has read or watched, is grounded on nothing. To see why anyone believes this, we start with the old puzzle underneath it.

Part 1 — the symbol grounding problem: how does a word get its meaning?

This is the deep root of the whole bet, so we go slowly and plainly.

Think about how a word means anything at all. In a language model, the word "hamburger" is stored as a pattern of numbers, and that pattern is shaped by which other words it appears near — "bun," "eat," "beef," "restaurant." But every one of those is also just a stored pattern shaped by its neighbours. Nowhere in the chain is there an actual hamburger — no smell, no taste, no warm weight in the hand. The philosopher Stevan Harnad named this the symbol grounding problem (the puzzle of how a symbol — a word or token — gets connected to the real thing it is about, instead of just to more symbols). His picture for it is exact: trying to learn the meaning of words from a dictionary alone, when you know none of the words, is a hopeless circle — each definition is made of more undefined words, and you spin forever without ever reaching the world. Meaning, Harnad argued, has to be grounded — anchored at the bottom in real sensing and acting, not floating on words all the way down.

The most famous stage for this argument is a thought-experiment called the Chinese Room, from the philosopher John Searle. Imagine a person locked in a room who does not speak Chinese, but who has a huge rulebook: Chinese symbols come in under the door, the person looks them up and, by the rules, writes other Chinese symbols to send back out. To the people outside, the answers look perfect — the room seems to "understand" Chinese. But the person inside understands nothing; they are just matching shapes by rules. Searle's point: a computer running a program is exactly like that person — it manipulates symbols by rules without understanding what any of them mean. Pure symbol-shuffling, however clever, is not understanding. (This is one of the most argued-over ideas in the study of the mind; there is no agreed answer. We use it here only for the one move it sets up next.)

The Robot Reply — meaning comes from a body

The most important answer to the Chinese Room, for us, is called the Robot Reply, and it is the seed of the whole embodiment bet. It concedes Searle's point about the sealed room — a symbol-shuffler with no contact with the world cannot understand — but says the fix is obvious: take it out of the room and give it a body. The encyclopedia states it cleanly:

"the Robot Reply suggests that we put a digital computer in a robot body, with sensors, such as video cameras and microphones, and add effectors, such as wheels to move around with, and arms with which to manipulate things in the world. Such a robot — a computer with a body — might do what a child does, learn by seeing and doing." (Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy, "The Chinese Room Argument")

(An effector is any part that acts on the world — a wheel, an arm, a gripper — the opposite of a sensor, which takes the world in.) Read the last line twice, because it is the heart of AP10: do what a child does, learn by seeing and doing. Once the machine can point its camera at a real hamburger, pick it up, and connect the word to the thing, the word stops being an empty symbol and gets a meaning — one earned through the body. Many serious thinkers have backed some form of this, including Harnad, Daniel Dennett, Jerry Fodor, and Hans Moravec. One AI textbook sums up the lesson bluntly: "embodied experience is necessary for the development of semantics" (semantics = real meaning — the link between a word and what it is about) (Carter 2007, as quoted in the encyclopedia). Harnad put the same demand as a question:

"Who is to say that the Turing Test … could be successfully passed without operations that draw on our sensory, motor, and other higher cognitive capacities as well?" (Stevan Harnad, as quoted in the Stanford Encyclopedia of Philosophy)

(The Turing Test is the old benchmark where a machine "passes" if it can chat well enough that a person cannot tell it from a human. Sensory = to do with the senses; motor = to do with movement.) His claim: you probably cannot really pass for a mind on words alone — the senses and the moving body are part of what thinking is. Harnad's word for the fix, grounding symbols in "robotic" functions that connect the system to the world, is where this whole page gets its name.

Hold on to the tension, though, because it drives the debate later. Searle himself was not convinced. His reply to the Robot Reply was that adding a camera changes nothing: "All the sensors can do is provide additional input to the computer — and it will be just syntactic input" (syntactic = to do with the form of symbols and the rules for shuffling them, with no meaning attached) (Searle, as summarised in the encyclopedia). In plain words: a picture from a camera is still just a stream of numbers — more symbols — so how could adding a body, by itself, turn number-shuffling into real understanding? That objection never fully goes away, and it comes back as this bet's sharpest doubt (Stuck #1). But first, what does the "give it a body" idea actually claim?

Part 2 — the bet in full: a mind is grown through a body

AP10's constructive claim — its answer to "so what should we build?" — has two parts: a claim about meaning and a claim about what is actually hard.

Claim 1 — meaning is sensorimotor, and the body does the thinking

The first part you now have: understanding is grounded in the sensorimotor loop — the endless cycle of act, sense the result, act again. You learn "heavy" by lifting, "slippery" by slipping, "behind" by walking around. On this view the body is not a mere delivery truck that carries data to a brain sitting apart from it; the body's way of moving and sensing shapes the concepts themselves. This is the school of thought called embodied cognition (the idea that thinking is not done by the brain alone, but by the whole brain-and-body acting in its world), which has gathered many supporters since the 1990s. Its boldest form came from the roboticist Rodney Brooks, who in 1991 argued that classic AI had it backwards. Old AI tried to give a robot a big inner symbolic model of the world and have it reason over the model. Brooks said: skip the model — let the robot sense the real world directly and react, moment to moment. His slogan became famous:

"The world is its own best model." (Rodney Brooks — his original phrasing was "It turns out to be better to use the world as its own model," in "Intelligence Without Representation," 1991; quoted via Brian Christian, The Alignment Problem.)

Unpack it, because it is the embodiment bet in five words. Why build a shaky little copy of the world inside the machine's head, keep it up to date, and reason over the copy — when the real world is right there, always perfectly accurate, ready to be sensed? Don't represent the world; stay in contact with it and act. Brooks built insect-like robots that walked over rough ground with almost no inner model at all, just tight sense-and-act loops — and they worked where the big-model robots fell over. His deeper point: a great deal of what looks like intelligence is not abstract reasoning at all, but this fast, wordless, bodily coping with a messy physical world — and that is the part old AI could not do.

Claim 2 — Moravec's paradox: the "easy" things are the hard things

That leads to the second, sharper part of the bet, named after the roboticist Hans Moravec. Look at what turned out to be easy and hard for machines, and you find a strange flip. The things we think of as the peak of human intelligence — playing chess, proving theorems, doing sums — turned out to be easy for computers; a machine beat the world chess champion back in 1997. But the things any toddler or animal does without a thought — walking across a room, recognising a face, picking up a cup you have never seen before — turned out to be fantastically hard, and are still not fully solved. This flip is Moravec's paradox (the finding that high-level reasoning needs little computing power, while low-level sensing and moving — the "easy" stuff — needs enormous amounts).

Why does this happen? Moravec's explanation is deep and worth holding. Evolution spent hundreds of millions of years perfecting perception and movement — that machinery is ancient, huge, and runs so smoothly we are not even aware of it. Abstract reasoning, by contrast, is a thin, recent trick, a few thousand years old, that we do slowly and badly. So the skills that feel effortless to us are actually the ones running on the most powerful, hardest-won machinery in nature — and the skills that feel like hard intelligence are the cheap, late add-on. [Established — Moravec's paradox is a widely-accepted observation, restated across AI for decades.] The lesson AP10 draws: the real core of intelligence was never the chess-and-maths part that text AI is good at. It is the sensing-and-moving part — the part you can only build with a body in a world. Language AI, by racing ahead on the "easy" reasoning while robots still struggle to fold a towel, is a live demonstration of exactly this paradox.

Putting both claims together, the bet is: build the machine a body, and let its intelligence grow from the bottom up — from sensing and acting in the physical world — because that grounded, sensorimotor base is what real understanding is made of, and it is the hard part everything else rests on.

Why this is a serious idea, not a fringe hunch

Three legs hold it up.

Leg 1 — the grounding argument is old, deep, and unanswered by scale

The first leg is the argument itself, and how well it has held up. The symbol grounding problem is not a fresh complaint invented to annoy today's chatbots; Harnad named it in 1990, and the worry underneath it — that shuffling symbols is not the same as understanding them — is older still. Decades of pure-symbol AI, and now a decade of ever-larger language models, have not made the question go away. And the everyday symptom is familiar: a language model can write a flawless paragraph about how to ride a bicycle and be utterly unable to ride one; it can define "left" and "right" perfectly and still get them muddled when asked to actually navigate. It handles the words about a thing far better than the thing itself. For a criticism to still bite after thirty-five years and a thousand-fold growth in the machines it targets is rare, and it is what keeps serious people taking embodiment seriously. [Established as a long-standing, unresolved challenge; whether a body is the only cure is the debate.]

Leg 2 — the join is now producing real robots: the "robot foundation model"

This is the concrete leg, and it has suddenly become strong. For most of its history embodiment was a philosophy with disappointing robots to show for it. That changed when researchers had a simple, powerful idea: take a big model already trained on internet text and images — a foundation model, the kind you met in AP1 (one big pretrained machine that everything else is built on) — and teach it to output robot actions as just another thing it can produce. Google's RT-2 (2023) was the landmark. Its paper's title states the whole plan:

"RT-2: Vision-Language-Action Models Transfer Web Knowledge to Robotic Control" (Brohan et al., Google DeepMind, 2023)

The trick is beautifully plain. A robot's action — move the arm this far, close the gripper this much — is turned into text-like tokens, and the model is trained to predict those the same way it predicts words:

"we express the actions as text tokens and incorporate them directly into the training set of the model in the same way as natural language tokens. We refer to such category of models as vision-language-action models (VLA)." (RT-2 paper, 2023)

(A token is a small chunk the model reads or writes — usually a piece of a word; here, a piece of an action. A vision-language-action model, or VLA, is the name for this kind of robot brain: it takes in vision (camera) and language (an instruction) and puts out action.) Why this matters so much: because the model was first trained on the whole web, it brings all that book-knowledge to the robot's body. RT-2 could follow an instruction it had never been trained on as a robot task — for example, "pick up the object that could be used as a hammer," and reach for a rock — because it read about hammers and rocks, and that knowledge now flowed into its hands. The paper calls these "emergent capabilities from Internet-scale training." This is the embodiment bet and the scale bet working together: web knowledge, given a body.

Since then this has become the hottest area in applied AI. [Established that VLAs exist and work; the specific systems and numbers below are dated snapshots — SOTA as of July 2026.] The open-source OpenVLA (2024) trained one model on 970,000 real robot episodes pooled from many labs (the Open X-Embodiment dataset), so one brain could drive many different robot bodies. Physical Intelligence's π0 (pronounced "pi-zero," late 2024) is widely treated as the leading generalist robot model: a single model with a few billion parameters, fine-tuned on a few thousand demonstrations, drove three different robot bodies through different jobs — a two-armed humanoid folded laundry, a wheeled robot unloaded a dishwasher, an arm on a desk assembled a box; its successors π0.5 and π0.7 push further on handling situations they were not trained on. Google DeepMind's Gemini Robotics 1.5 (2025) is a VLA that "thinks before acting" — it works out intermediate steps in words before it moves — and can transfer a skill learned on one robot body to another. And a wave of humanoid robot companies — Figure (its "Helix" brain), Tesla (Optimus), 1X, Apptronik, Agility, with Nvidia's open GR00T models underneath many — moved out of pure demos into early paid pilots in factories and warehouses through 2025–26. After decades of promises, robots that learn like large models are, at last, doing real work.

Leg 3 — every mind we actually know of is embodied

The third leg is the quiet, hard-to-argue-with one: look at the only real general intelligences in existence, and every single one has a body. People, dogs, crows, octopuses — each grew its mind by moving and sensing in a physical world, none by reading a text dump. A human child, the clearest case, does not learn what a ball is from a caption; it learns by grabbing, dropping, throwing, and chasing — thousands of small experiments with its own hands. This connects AP10 to two bets you have already seen. It is the natural partner of AP5's world model: AP5 says learn the world by watching it; AP10 says the watching only fully works when you can also act and see your action's effect. And it is the body that AP6's brain-based bet says the neocortex was built for — recall that one of Hawkins' four must-haves for a real mind is learning through movement (a mind learns by moving its sensors and predicting what it will feel next, not by sitting still and being fed data). Three different approaches — world models, brain-based, and embodiment — all point at the same missing piece: a body that acts. When independent lines of argument converge on one gap, that gap is worth taking seriously. [Likely — the biological case is strong; that it proves a body is strictly required for a machine is contested, see Stuck #1.]

So what does AP10 say intelligence is?

Pulling the legs together, here is AP10's answer to "what is intelligence?":

  • Intelligence is grounded, sensorimotor competence — the ability to perceive a real, messy physical world and act skilfully in it, built from the bottom up through the loop of moving and sensing. Not a stream of correct symbols, but real dealing-with-the-world.
  • What it optimises is real-world perception-and-action performance — can the system actually do the physical task, in the physical world, including cases it never saw? Not a score on a text exam, but competence in a body.
  • Its claim about the missing piece: what pure-text (and even pure-video) AI lacks is grounding through action. Its symbols float on other symbols; it has read about the world but never touched it, so its "understanding" has no anchor at the bottom. Give it a body — senses plus the power to act, and the chance to learn from what its actions cause — and its knowledge finally connects to reality. And by Moravec's paradox, that grounded sensorimotor base is not a small add-on; it is the hard core of intelligence that everything else was built on top of.

That is the bet. Now let us judge it.

Judging the bet: where it is stuck

Be fair first. AP10 owns one of the oldest and deepest challenges in the field — the symbol grounding problem — which pure-symbol AI has never cleanly answered; it has Moravec's paradox, a real and repeatedly-confirmed pattern; and, after decades of thin results, it can now point to robots that genuinely learn and work (Leg 2). Hold that. Now the four places it is truly stuck.

Stuck #1 — necessity vs sufficiency: does a mind need a body, or is that just one route?

This is AP10's deepest weakness, and it is now a live, sharp fight rather than an old philosophical one. The plain fact in the way of the bet: today's most capable AI has no body at all, and it got surprisingly far anyway — it reasons, writes code, passes hard exams, and holds a conversation, all from text and pictures. If a mind truly needed a body to mean anything, how did a bodiless machine get this good? The embodiment camp's answer splits the field into two:

  • Computational functionalists say intelligence is just information processing, and it does not matter what stuff (a body, a brain, a chip) does the processing — so a body is one way to get grounded data, not a requirement.
  • Embodied-cognition people say no — real understanding must be earned through a body, and the text machines only look smart.

There is a sharp answer to "how did the bodiless machine get so good," and honesty demands stating it: the language model is riding on our bodies. Every sentence it trained on was written by humans who did have bodies and did ground their words in real experience. So the model inherits a kind of second-hand grounding — it "knows" a hamburger is warm and heavy because millions of embodied humans wrote sentences carrying that fact. One 2026 paper calls this borrowing a sort of epistemic parasitism — the model lives off meaning that grounded humans already paid for. That reframes the whole quarrel and points at its sharpest version, argued in a 2026 paper titled "Intelligence Requires Grounding But Not Embodiment": maybe symbols really do need grounding — a connection to the world — but that grounding could come from video, images, and human data, without the machine ever having its own body. If that is right, AP10 has confused two different things: grounding (probably necessary) and a physical body (maybe not). That distinction — grounding vs embodiment — is the crux the whole bet now turns on, and it is unsettled. [Contested — the key live dispute, July 2026.]

Stuck #2 — the data problem: you cannot download experience

This is AP10's most practical wall, and it is a hard one. The scale bet (AP1) worked because text is abundant and free — the whole internet was just sitting there to be read. Robot experience is the opposite. There is no internet of robot actions to scrape; every example of "this arm movement, in this situation, had this result" has to be collected in the real world, one slow, breakable, expensive robot-hour at a time. This is the same sample-inefficiency you met in AP4 (learning by trial and error needs a huge number of tries — cheap in a fast simulator, but very slow in the real world), now made even worse because the tries happen at real-world speed with hardware that wears out and breaks. Two escape routes are tried, and each has its own gap:

  • Simulation — practise millions of times inside a fast physics video-game, then move to the real robot. But a simulator is never quite reality: objects, light, and especially contact (how a soft or bendy thing behaves when touched) come out subtly wrong, so a skill learned in the sim often fails on the real robot. This mismatch is the sim-to-real gap (the drop in performance when a robot trained in simulation meets the messier real world).
  • Human video — learn by watching people do the task on video, of which there is plenty. But a human hand is not a robot gripper; the shapes and joints do not match, so copying a human motion often does not transfer to the robot's very different body — the human-to-robot gap.

So the bet that promised to get past the data wall (Leg 2) runs straight into a different, harder data wall of its own. As of 2026 this — not clever model design — is widely seen as the true bottleneck for embodiment. [Established — the data/sim-to-real bottleneck is the field's most-cited practical obstacle, July 2026.]

Stuck #3 — the Bitter Lesson again: maybe scale just absorbs the body

This is the same threat that shadows AP7, AP3, and AP8, pointed now at embodiment. Recall the Bitter Lesson from AP1: across AI's history, hand-built special structure keeps losing to methods that just learn from more data and compute. Now look closely at the winning robots of Leg 2 — RT-2, π0, Gemini Robotics. They are not built on some special "embodied" architecture. They are ordinary large models — the very same vision-and-language machines from the scale bet — with robot actions bolted on as extra tokens and fine-tuned on some robot data. So a critic can say the recent robot progress does not prove that embodiment is a distinct road to intelligence; it proves the opposite — that scaling ordinary foundation models, then adding a body at the end, is what works. On this reading a body is not the source of the mind; it is a cheap output attached to a mind that scale already built. If that holds, "embodiment" is not a rival path to AGI at all — it is one application of the scale path, the last mile (the short final step) where a general model is connected to a motor. [Contested — a genuine and uncomfortable tension: the robots that vindicate embodiment are built out of the bet it argues against.]

Stuck #4 — "embodiment" is a slippery target, hard to pin down and hard to disprove

The last crack is about the shape of the claim. What exactly counts as "a body"? A steel humanoid with hands, clearly — but what about a robot arm bolted to a table? A self-driving car? A character with a simulated body inside a video-game world, which has senses and can act but is made of code? Each has some embodiment, and supporters can wave any success through as proof while explaining any failure as "not embodied enough yet." A claim that can absorb every outcome is hard to falsify (to prove wrong by a clear test), and a claim you cannot test risks explaining nothing. There is also a softer version of the whole bet that quietly gives up its boldest form: almost everyone now agrees a body helps and adds useful grounded data — but "a body helps" is a mild, easy claim, a world away from the strong one that says a body is necessary and is the true seat of intelligence. When pressed, the bet often retreats from the strong claim to the mild one — and the mild one no longer names a distinct road to AGI. [Contested — the vagueness/falsifiability worry is fair; supporters answer that specific robot benchmarks are concrete, testable targets.]

The big question under all of these

Every doubt above is one question: is a physical body a necessary source of general intelligence — the thing that gives symbols their meaning and grows the mind from the bottom up — or is it just a useful output device, a convenient way to collect grounded data and act, that a big enough model (trained on our already-grounded text and video) can drive without needing to have grown up in a body itself? AP10 says the body is the source: no grounding without acting, and the sensorimotor base is the hard core of the mind. The other side says the body is an output: grounding can come second-hand through data, the hard core is the general model, and the robot is just where that model reaches out and touches the world. Notice the exact echo of its neighbours: this is the same shape as AP7's "is symbolic structure a real ingredient or a crutch scale absorbs?" and AP3's "is the scaffolding a road or an app layer?" — the one recurring fight of this whole map: is the extra thing (symbols, agents, a body) a true missing ingredient, or something scale will fold in? As of July 2026 this is genuinely open — and because robots are now real products, it is being decided by evidence, fast. [Contested — the key open question.]

⚠️ Honesty box

  • The critique is stronger than the cure. Symbols floating on other symbols are not grounded is old, deep, and never cleanly answered (Leg 1). But "therefore a mind needs a physical body" does not follow by itself — a bodiless model got very capable by riding on our grounded text (Stuck #1). Keep the sharp diagnosis apart from the strong remedy. [Contested.]
  • The best wins may belong to the rival bet. The impressive new robots (RT-2, π0, Gemini Robotics) are real (Leg 2) — but they are ordinary large models with actions added, not proof that embodiment is its own road (Stuck #3). They show a body can be driven well; they do not show the body is what makes the mind. [Established win, contested meaning.]
  • The data wall it escapes is replaced by a harder one. Embodiment promises fresh experience past the text data wall (Leg 2) — but real-world robot data is slow, costly, and breakable, and the sim-to-real and human-to-robot gaps are unsolved (Stuck #2). The fuel problem does not disappear; it changes shape and gets worse. [Established.]
  • "A body helps" and "a body is necessary" are very different bets. The mild claim (grounded data helps) is almost surely true and widely shared. The strong claim (only a body grounds a real mind) is the interesting one — and it is exactly the one now in doubt (Stuck #1, #4). Watch which one a claim is really making. [Contested.]
  • Numbers and names age fast. RT-2, π0's three-body demo, Gemini Robotics 1.5, the 2025–26 humanoid pilots (Figure, Optimus, 1X, Apptronik, Nvidia GR00T) — these are dated snapshots, and the humanoid field in particular is full of marketing hype; several high-profile demos have been shown to lean on hidden human tele-operation (a person secretly steering the robot by remote). Treat specific robots and scores as this-month facts. The lasting parts are the symbol grounding problem, the Robot Reply, Moravec's paradox, "the world is its own best model," the VLA / robot-foundation-model idea, the sim-to-real / data bottleneck, and the grounding-vs-embodiment question. [Established core, dated specifics.]

How to use this (if you want to direct AI work)

  • Ask what a claimed "understanding" is grounded in. When a system is said to "understand" a physical task, ask whether it has ever acted in that domain or only read about it. A model that can describe a task perfectly may fail utterly at doing it — the words-about-a-thing / the-thing gap. For anything physical, trust demonstrated action, not fluent description.
  • Separate grounding from embodiment — they are different levers. If you need real-world meaning, you need grounding (a link to real data — video, sensors, logs). You do not automatically need a robot body. Reach for a full physical body only when the task truly requires acting in the world; often grounded data plus a general model is cheaper and enough (Stuck #1).
  • Respect the data cost before betting on robots. The hard part of a physical-AI project is almost never the model; it is getting enough real-world experience. Budget for the slow, breakable, expensive business of collecting robot data, and check any plan's honest answer to the sim-to-real and human-to-robot gaps (Stuck #2). A demo in simulation is not a working robot.
  • Watch for the Bitter Lesson in your own robot stack. If your edge is hand-built "embodied" special structure, ask whether the next general model, fine-tuned on a bit of action data, will simply absorb it (Stuck #3). Prefer approaches that get stronger as general models improve over custom-built structure that scale may soon make pointless.
  • Discount humanoid demos by default. The humanoid field is the most hype-heavy corner of AI right now. Before believing a demo, ask: was it autonomous or tele-operated? one lucky take or a reliable rate? a narrow scripted task or genuine generalisation? (July 2026: fleets are in the hundreds, tasks are narrow, and full autonomy is still rare.)
  • What you hand to others: building the robot, wiring the sensors, tuning the control policy. What you keep for yourself: deciding whether a task truly needs a body or just grounded data, refusing to trust described-but-never-done competence, respecting the real-world data cost, and holding the open question — whether the body is the source of a mind or just its hands (Stuck #1).

Connections

  • Keep only three things: ① AP10 = a mind needs a body. Today's AI learned from symbols alone, and symbols defined only by other symbols never touch the real world — the symbol grounding problem (Harnad). Real meaning, this bet says, is sensorimotor: earned by acting on the world and sensing what changes, the way a child learns "heavy" by lifting. And by Moravec's paradox, that sensing-and-moving part — not chess and maths — is the hard core of intelligence. ② The modern form is the robot foundation model / VLA (vision-language-action): take a big model trained on the web and teach it to output robot actions as tokens, so web knowledge flows into a body (RT-2, π0, Gemini Robotics). ③ It is stuck on four cracks: necessity vs sufficiency (a bodiless model got very capable by riding on our grounded text — maybe grounding is needed but a body is not), the data problem (no internet of actions; sim-to-real and human-to-robot gaps), the Bitter Lesson (the winning robots are ordinary large models with actions bolted on — so is embodiment a road or just scale's last mile?), and it is hard to falsify ("body" is a slippery word; "a body helps" is not the strong claim).
  • Down the ladder (already read): guessing the next word · scaling laws & the data wall — the pure-symbol, no-body machine AP10 says is ungrounded.
  • Its closest partner: AP5 · world models / JEPA — AP5 says watch the world; AP10 says act on it. They share LeCun's "text is the wrong food" and the sensory-bandwidth point, and both say the missing piece is contact with the real world. AP10 adds the body and the action.
  • Its other siblings: AP6 · brain-based — its "learning through movement" is embodiment seen from the brain's side. AP4 · RL from interaction — a robot learning by trial and error is RL in a body, and inherits its sample-inefficiency (Stuck #2). AP3 · agents — a robot is the embodied agent AP3's software agents point toward. AP1 · scale — the pure-scale bet, whose Bitter Lesson is AP10's chief threat (Stuck #3), yet whose foundation models power every winning robot (Leg 2).
  • How sure are we? The symbol grounding problem, Moravec's paradox, and that VLAs work — [Established / Likely]. That a physical body is necessary for a general mind (rather than one useful route to grounded data), that embodiment names a distinct road rather than scale's last mile, and that the data bottleneck can be beaten — [Contested, open].

Check yourself (try one, from memory)

  1. Say the AP10 bet in one plain sentence, using the words body, act, and grounded.
  2. What is the symbol grounding problem? Use the "dictionary in a language you don't know" picture to explain why words-about-words may not be enough.
  3. Explain the Robot Reply to the Chinese Room. What does it say fixes the sealed symbol-shuffler — and what was Searle's comeback?
  4. State Moravec's paradox, and explain why AP10 thinks it points to embodiment as the hard core of intelligence.
  5. What is a VLA (vision-language-action model), and how did RT-2 get web knowledge into a robot's actions?
  6. Give the difference between grounding and embodiment (Stuck #1), and say why the difference matters for whether AP10 is right.

id: c-ap11-whole-brain-emulation sortkey: 2011 title: AP11 · Whole-brain emulation — the "copy it without understanding it" bet domains: [frontier, approaches-to-agi] level: core prereqs: [c-next-word, c-scaling-laws, c-ap1-scale, c-ap6-brain-based] provides: [whole-brain-emulation-the-bet, mind-uploading, connectome-connectomics, scan-translate-simulate, connectome-is-not-a-running-brain, scale-separation-assumption, substrate-independence, the-copy-problem] resources: [] status: ready reading_time: 33 min rev: 1 created: 2026-07-16 updated: 2026-07-16

AP11 · Whole-brain emulation — the "copy it without understanding it" bet

This is the eleventh and last big idea we look at for how to build a machine that can think in a general way — and it is the strangest of them all. Every other bet tries to work out what intelligence is and then build it: copy human text (AP1), think longer (AP2), chase reward (AP4), model the world (AP5), search for programs (AP8), copy the brain's method (AP6). This one gives up on understanding entirely. It says: we have one machine that is already generally intelligent — a human brain — so stop trying to figure out how it works. Instead, scan a real brain in complete detail, down to every cell and every connection, and copy that exact structure into a computer. Run the copy, and — if the scan was faithful enough — a mind wakes up inside the machine, thinking the same way the original did, whether or not anyone understands why. This is the most literal "copy the brain" bet, and the most far-off: it needs scanning and computing powers far beyond anything we have. But it is a real, funded scientific effort, and it raises the deepest questions on the whole map — about what a copied mind is, and whether making one is even allowed. This page explains it all from zero: the bet in one minute, how "copy without understanding" is supposed to work, how far the copying has actually got, why serious people take it seriously, and the four places it is stuck.

You are here: this is the AP11 page — the eleventh and final bet on the "approaches to AGI" map (see APPROACHES_TO_AGI), and the eleventh (last) approach card written in full — so this page completes the map. AGI means artificial general intelligence — a machine that can think across many different problems, not just one. The short name for this bet is whole-brain emulation (emulation = an exact working copy of one system running inside another; here, a copy of a real brain running inside a computer), often also called mind uploading (the popular name for moving — or rather copying — a mind from a brain onto a computer).

This page builds on rungs of the ladder you have already climbed, all short and plain: guessing the next word — how today's AI learns from text; scaling laws & the data wall — the steady curve scale rides; AP1 · the "make it bigger" bet — the mainstream text-and-scale bet that this one is the complete opposite of; and above all AP6 · brain-based — its closest cousin and its opposite twin, where you met the idea of copying the brain. A one-line reminder of each is given where it is used, so you will not get lost. AP11 is the deep home for whole-brain emulation, mind uploading, and the connectome.

Where the facts come from: the durable idea is grounded in Sandberg & Bostrom's "Whole Brain Emulation: A Roadmap" (2008, Future of Humanity Institute, Oxford) — the founding document that laid the bet out carefully. The state of the real copying work is grounded in the published connectome maps (the worm, the fruit fly, and specks of mouse and human brain) and the big brain-simulation projects. A fresh check of the field, done on the web (as of July 2026), covers the newest maps, scanning methods, and funded efforts. Because this bet moves slowly but its specifics still age, each fast-moving number is dated below.

The bet in one minute

Here is the whole idea, as short as it goes.

Every other bet on this map is a theory — a guess about what intelligence really is, turned into a thing to build. This bet refuses to guess. It points at the one machine we already agree is generally intelligent — a human brain — and says: we do not need a theory of it. We just need to copy it. Take a real brain, measure its physical structure completely — every one of its billions of cells, and every one of the roughly one hundred trillion tiny connections between them — and build a model of exactly that structure inside a computer. Then switch the model on. Each simulated cell behaves like the real cell it was copied from; the whole copied network then does what the real brain did — it perceives, remembers, decides, talks. A mind runs in the machine. And here is the key, almost unbelievable, claim: this could work even though no one understands how the brain produces a mind. You are not building intelligence; you are photocopying it. The picture does not need to understand the page. The champions of this bet are a small community of neuroscientists, computer scientists, and futurists who study "connectomics" and mind uploading.

Why would anyone believe copying-without-understanding could work? Because of one simple idea: a faithful enough copy behaves like the original for the same reason the original does — automatically. If you rebuilt a car engine part for part from an exact blueprint, it would run, even if you had no idea why engines run. The bet is that a brain is the same: get the copy detailed enough, and thinking happens too, as a side effect of the structure being right. You never have to solve the mystery of intelligence. You just have to be an extremely good copier.

That is the bet. The rest of this page explains how "copy without understanding" is supposed to work (the three steps), how far the real copying has actually got (from a worm to a speck of human brain), why serious people back it, and why it might still be wrong.

First, a one-line reminder of the base

Three quick reminders from rungs you have already read, so this page stands on its own.

  • From guessing the next word and AP1: today's main AI is a neural network trained to guess the next word in human text. A neural network here means a huge web of simple number-units, loosely inspired by brain cells but not a real copy of any brain. AP11 is the far end away from this: not "loosely inspired by the brain," but "an exact copy of one real brain." (New to those? Read those two rungs first; they are short.)
  • From AP6 · brain-based: that bet also said copy the brain — but it meant something quite different. AP6 (Jeff Hawkins) wants to understand the brain's method (the one repeated "cortical algorithm" — the single basic recipe the thinking part of the brain runs, copied about 150,000 times) and then rebuild that method in a machine, throwing away the messy biology. Hold that idea tight, because AP11 is its opposite twin, and the next paragraph turns on the difference.
  • Also from AP6: the strongest reason to look at the brain at all is that it is the only thing we know for certain is generally intelligent — it learns almost anything, from little, on about 20 watts of power (a dim light bulb's worth). Both AP6 and AP11 start from this one fact. They split on what to do with it.

Now the one new idea this page adds — and it is a single, sharp split from AP6. Picture a brilliant old clock you want to reproduce. AP6 is the watchmaker: it opens the clock, studies the gears until it understands the mechanism, then builds a new clock its own way — maybe out of different parts. AP11 is the photocopier: it does not open the clock or understand anything; it measures the exact position of every single part and makes a perfect physical duplicate. AP6 needs to understand the brain's algorithm — and, as you saw, its biggest problem is that nobody knows that algorithm yet. AP11 needs to understand nothing — only to copy accurately enough. That is the whole bet in one line: you can have a working mind without any theory of how minds work, if you are willing to copy an existing one in full detail. Everything below is about whether that is possible, and how far off it is.

Part 1 — how "copy without understanding" is supposed to work

This is the heart of the bet, so we go slowly and plainly. The founding statement of it is from a careful 2008 report by two Oxford researchers, Anders Sandberg and Nick Bostrom, called "Whole Brain Emulation: A Roadmap." They set out the whole idea in one clean sentence:

"The basic idea is to take a particular brain, scan its structure in detail, and construct a software model of it that is so faithful to the original that, when run on appropriate hardware, it will behave in essentially the same way as the original brain." (Sandberg & Bostrom, "Whole Brain Emulation: A Roadmap," 2008)

Unpack it slowly, because every word carries weight. "A particular brain" — one specific real person's brain, not a general design. "Scan its structure in detail" — measure its physical make-up completely, cell by cell. "A software model … so faithful … it will behave in essentially the same way" — the copy in the computer acts like the original brain because it is the original brain's structure, rebuilt. (Software = the instructions and data a computer runs, as opposed to the physical machine; hardware = the physical machine itself. A model here = a working copy inside the computer, not a description of it.)

Now the deep part, the part that makes AP11 different from every other bet on the map. Sandberg and Bostrom's central argument is this: whole-brain emulation, unlike every other route to machine intelligence, does not require us to understand intelligence. Their reasoning is worth holding carefully. To build a mind from scratch (the AP1–AP10 way), you must have some working theory of what a mind is — you need to know why the parts you are building produce thinking. But to copy a mind, you do not. You only need two things: (1) a complete map of the brain's low-level parts and how they are wired, and (2) the local rules for how each part behaves moment to moment — how one brain cell reacts to a signal from the cells connected to it. Get those two things right at the small scale, run them all together, and the high-level thinking emerges on its own — for the same reason it emerges in the real brain. You never have to answer the big question "how does a brain make a mind?" at all. As the roadmap puts it, this is the one path where you might reach a thinking machine without any real understanding of intelligence.

That is a genuinely strange and powerful idea, so let me name the exact reason it might work — and the exact thing it is quietly assuming.

The one big assumption: "scale separation"

Copying-without-understanding rests on a single hopeful assumption, and the whole bet lives or dies by it. It is called scale separation (the idea that the small scale and the large scale can be "separated" — that below a certain level of fine detail, nothing more matters for how the brain behaves). In plain words: the claim is that there is some level of detail that is "enough" — copy the brain down to that level, and everything finer than it (the exact wobble of individual atoms, say) makes no difference to the resulting mind, so you can safely ignore it.

Why this matters so much: a real brain is detailed all the way down, forever — cells, inside them molecules, inside them atoms, inside them still-smaller physics. You can never copy infinite detail. So the bet only works if you can stop somewhere — if there is a floor below which the details are just noise that does not matter. Sandberg and Bostrom think that floor probably exists and is somewhere around the level of the connections between cells (perhaps with some chemistry added). But — and this is the honest hole in the plan — nobody yet knows where the floor is, or even for certain that there is one. That single unknown is the source of most of the trouble later on this page. Hold it.

The three steps

Given the idea, the plan itself is simple to state (and extremely hard to do). It has three steps, in order:

  1. Scan. Take a brain and measure its complete physical structure — where every cell is, and every connection between cells. Today the only way to do this at the needed fineness destroys the brain: you freeze it, slice it into unimaginably thin sheets, and photograph each sheet with a powerful microscope. (This is called destructive scanning — the original brain does not survive the process. Whether a non-destructive scan of a living brain will ever be possible is an open question.)
  2. Translate. Turn that mountain of pictures into a working map: trace every cell through the stack of images, find every connection, and record its properties. This step is now done mostly by AI, because there is far too much to do by hand. The finished map is called a connectome (a complete "wiring diagram" of a brain or nervous system — every cell and every connection; the word is built like "genome," but for connections). The field that builds these is connectomics.
  3. Simulate. Load the connectome into a computer, give each simulated cell the rules for how a real cell behaves, and run the whole thing forward in time. If scale separation held — if you copied down to a fine-enough level — a mind now runs in the machine.

Keep those three words: scan, translate, simulate. They are the spine of the whole approach, and — as the next part shows — the world has only barely begun the first two, and has never truly done the third for any real mind.

Part 2 — how far the copying has actually got

This bet is not about ideas; it is about doing an unimaginably hard copy job. So the fair question is: how far along is it, really? The honest answer is a ladder that climbs from a worm to a speck — and then faces an enormous jump. Every number below is a dated snapshot (as of July 2026); connectomics is moving, so treat the specifics as this-year facts, not permanent ones.

The connectome ladder — real, and genuinely climbing

  • The worm — C. elegans (302 brain cells). A tiny roundworm's entire nervous system — just 302 neurons and about 7,000 connections — was fully mapped by hand back in 1986. (A neuron is a single brain cell; a synapse is one connection point where a signal passes from one neuron to the next — reminders from AP6.) This is the simplest complete connectome there is, and it has been finished for about forty years.
  • The fruit fly (about 140,000 brain cells). In October 2024, a large team (the FlyWire project, ~200 people across ~50 labs) published the first complete connectome of an adult fruit fly brain in the journal Nature: about 140,000 neurons and more than 50 million connections. This was a landmark — the first whole-brain wiring diagram of a creature that flies, walks, and learns.
  • A speck of mouse brain. In April 2025, the MICrONS project (Allen Institute, Baylor, Princeton) mapped one cubic millimetre of a mouse's brain — a piece the size of a grain of sand — and found about 84,000 neurons and over 500 million connections in that speck, plus, remarkably, a record of the living cells' activity before the tissue was cut. It is the most detailed map of a piece of mammal brain ever made.
  • A speck of human brain. A similar one-cubic-millimetre piece of human brain has been mapped (a Harvard–Google effort): about 16,000 neurons and 150 million connections in that one tiny fleck.

That is real progress, and it is speeding up, helped by better microscopes and by AI doing the tracing. So the copying is not science fiction; it is a live, funded science. (For scale of effort: in 2023 the US NIH began a $150 million program, "BRAIN CONNECTS," just to build better connectome-mapping tools.)

…and then the enormous jump

Now stand back and look at the size of the last step. A whole human brain has about 86 billion neurons — not thousands, not millions, but tens of billions — and on the order of 100 trillion connections. The best human map so far covers one cubic millimetre. A whole human brain is well over a million times larger than that. And that is only the scanning — a job that today destroys the brain, takes years for a single speck, and would produce an amount of data measured in hundreds of exabytes (an exabyte is a billion billion bytes; hundreds of exabytes is a sizeable fraction of all the data humanity currently stores — for one brain).

Then you still have to run the copy. Estimates of the computing power needed to simulate a whole human brain in real time run from roughly 10^18 operations per second (a top supercomputer of today) up to numbers many trillions of times larger — the honest range spans about fifteen zeros of uncertainty, because, again, nobody knows how much fine detail you must include (the scale-separation question from Part 1, which now becomes a real problem). [Established that the scale is enormous and the required detail is unknown; the exact numbers are contested estimates, as of July 2026.]

So the state of AP11 in one line: the first rungs of the ladder are real and being climbed, but the top of the ladder — a whole human brain, scanned, translated, and actually run as a mind — is not close. No one has yet fully simulated even the 302-neuron worm as a living, learning creature (more on that hard fact in Stuck #1). AP11 is a real road with its first steps really taken — and a destination still a very long way off.

Why this is a serious idea, not just science fiction

Given how far off it is, why do careful people study it at all? Three legs hold it up.

Leg 1 — it is the one bet that needs no theory of intelligence

This is AP11's unique strength, and it is real. Every other approach on this map can fail simply by being wrong about what intelligence is — scale might not be enough (AP1), reward might not be enough (AP4), the brain's algorithm might never be found (AP6). AP11 sidesteps that entire risk. It does not propose a theory of intelligence that could be mistaken; it proposes to copy a working example, and a faithful copy works for the same reason the original works — no theory required. Sandberg and Bostrom stress that this gives the bet something the others lack: a clear, well-defined target. With most routes to AI we are not even sure what the milestones are. With emulation the milestones are obvious and physical — scan better, translate better, simulate more cells. You may not reach the target for a long time, but you always know exactly what "closer" means. A hard problem with a clear finish line is a very different, and more hopeful, thing than a vague one. [Established — the "no theory needed, clear target" property is the bet's genuine and distinctive advantage.]

Leg 2 — the copying is really happening, and accelerating

This is the concrete leg. For most of its history emulation was pure speculation. That has changed. The connectome ladder in Part 2 is real published science: a fly brain fully mapped in 2024, specks of mouse and human brain mapped in 2025, each with genuine cell-and-synapse detail. The enabling tools are improving fast — for example expansion microscopy (a clever trick that physically swells the brain tissue in a water-absorbing gel, like a sponge, so that ordinary microscopes can suddenly see tiny, synapse-sized details), which by 2025 could resolve features as small as 20 nanometres (a nanometre is a millionth of a millimetre) — and AI now does the once-impossible job of tracing billions of cells through the image stacks. Progress that was flat for decades is now clearly moving. A bet whose first two steps are being demonstrated, at rising speed, on real brains is a serious scientific program, not a daydream. [Established that connectomics is real and accelerating; that it will ever reach a whole human brain is unproven — see Stuck #2.]

Leg 3 — the existence proof, plus "substrate independence"

The third leg is shared with AP6 and made sharper. Start from the one hard fact: the brain is the only general intelligence we know exists — so copying it is the one route we are certain aims at a real target, not a mirage. Now add the assumption that makes copying-into-a-computer even thinkable: substrate independence (the idea that a mind depends on the pattern of activity — the information being processed — not on the stuff it runs on; so the same pattern, run on brain cells or on silicon chips, would be the same mind). If mind is what the brain does (the computation) rather than what the brain is made of (the wet biology), then re-running that same activity on a computer should re-create the same mind. This is the same family of thinking as the computational functionalism you met in AP10 · embodiment (short reminder: the view that intelligence is just information-processing, and any machine that does the same processing has the same mind, whatever it is built from). If that view is right, then a faithful enough copy of a brain, running on a chip, would not merely act like the person — it would, in the fullest sense, be a mind. [Likely as the mainstream scientific view; that a copy would truly be conscious is contested — see Stuck #4.]

So what does AP11 say intelligence is?

Pulling the legs together, here is AP11's answer to "what is intelligence?":

  • Intelligence is a particular physical pattern of activity — the specific dance of billions of connected cells that a real brain performs. Not a formula, not an algorithm we can write down, not a theory — just this exact working structure, whatever it turns out to be.
  • How it is built is by exact copying, not by understanding, designing, or growing. You reproduce the pattern in full detail and let the mind result from getting the structure right.
  • Its claim about the missing piece: what every from-scratch approach lacks is a correct, complete theory of intelligence — and maybe no such tidy theory exists, or maybe we are decades from finding it. AP11 says: you do not need one. The complete answer to "how does intelligence work?" is already written down — inside every human head — in a form we can, in principle, read off and copy without ever decoding it.

That is the bet. Now let us judge it.

Judging the bet: where it is stuck

Be fair first. AP11 owns two things no other bet does: it needs no possibly-wrong theory of intelligence (Leg 1), and it aims at the one system we know is generally intelligent (Leg 3); and its first steps are real, accelerating science (Leg 2). Hold that. Now the four places it is truly stuck — and for this bet, they are steep.

Stuck #1 — a wiring diagram is not a running brain

This is AP11's deepest technical problem, and the tiny worm makes it painfully clear. Recall that C. elegans, with only 302 neurons, has had its complete connectome — its full wiring diagram — for about forty years. You would think that with the whole map in hand, simulating the worm as a living creature would be easy. It is not. No one has done it. As of 2026 the most advanced attempt (a project called BAAIWorm) can reproduce some basic wriggling movement using fixed, guessed-at connection strengths — but it cannot learn, and it is not the worm.

Why does having the full map not give you the running animal? Because a connectome tells you that two cells are connected, but almost nothing about how. Think of it this way: a connectome is like an electrical wiring diagram with all the components rubbed out — every wire is drawn, but every switch, battery, and bulb is a blank box. The map does not tell you whether a connection makes the next cell fire more or less, how strong the push is, how fast the signal travels, which of many chemical messengers it uses (a neurotransmitter — the chemical a neuron releases to signal the next one; there are many kinds, and which one is used changes everything), or how all of that shifts from second to second as the brain learns. A real brain runs on this constantly-changing chemical and electrical detail, and the static wiring diagram captures none of it. The connectome is the easy part — and it is already extraordinarily hard. The truly hard part is measuring the live, moving inner state of every cell, which today we cannot read at anything like the needed scale. This is scale separation (Part 1) turning from a hopeful assumption into a wall: we do not know how much of this fine detail is essential, and every drop we must include makes the whole job vastly harder. [Established — that a connectome alone is far from sufficient for emulation is a central, well-known result; the worm is the standing proof.]

Stuck #2 — the scale is beyond anything we can do, and the best-funded tries under-delivered

This is the practical wall, and it is huge. Part 2 laid out the arithmetic: a whole human brain is over a million times bigger than the largest speck yet mapped; scanning it (today, destructively) would take a very long time and produce hundreds of exabytes of data; running the copy could need computing power thousands of billions of times beyond today's largest machines — with the true figure unknown to within fifteen zeros. None of these is a small gap to be closed with a bit more effort; each is a huge one.

And the cautionary history is real. The two most ambitious, best-funded attempts to simulate brain tissue both fell far short of their promises. Europe's Human Brain Project — funded at around one billion euros over a decade (2013–2023) — promised a simulated human brain and did not deliver one; hundreds of neuroscientists signed an open letter of protest, and the project was reorganized away from that goal. Switzerland's Blue Brain Project, which built detailed simulations of small pieces of rat brain, ended in December 2024, having simulated on the order of tens of thousands of neurons — impressive, but an extremely small step toward 86 billion. Critics of both argued the same thing: simulating brain tissue was attempted before we understood enough to know what to simulate — you can build a giant model and still not have a mind, or even much insight. The lesson is serious: even with major money and talent, the road proved far longer than its supporters hoped. [Established — the scale gap is enormous and the flagship simulation projects under-delivered, as of 2026.]

Stuck #3 — even if it worked, it may be the slowest path, and it teaches you nothing

Now suppose, generously, that all of this is solved one far-off day. There is still a strange double problem, unique to AP11.

First, it is almost certainly the slowest road on the map. The scanning, translating, and simulating powers it needs are so far beyond today's that the mainstream bets — scale (AP1), reasoning (AP2) — will very likely reach AGI decades before emulation matures. This is AP6's "right but too slow" worry in its most extreme form: even if copying a brain would work in principle, some faster approach almost surely gets to a general machine first, and by then a slow, exact human copy is a scientific curiosity, not the way the future was built.

Second — and this is the deep one — AP11 is the one bet that, even in total success, hands you no understanding. Every other approach, if it works, teaches you something: a theory of reasoning, of reward, of world-models, of the cortical algorithm. You end up knowing how intelligence works, and so you can improve it, scale it, redesign it. AP11 hands you a working mind you understand no better than before — a sealed box you copied but cannot read. (A sealed box, sometimes called a "black box," = a system you can run and watch from outside but whose inner workings you cannot see into or explain.) You would have made a digital person without learning the secret of minds. And a copied human mind is, by definition, roughly human-level — not obviously the superhuman intelligence the rest of the map is chasing (though a copy could in principle be run faster, paused, or duplicated, which brings its own strangeness). So AP11's very selling point — no understanding required — is also its ceiling: no understanding produced. [Likely — the "slowest path, and yields a mind we still can't explain" critique is a serious and distinctive limit.]

Stuck #4 — the questions no other bet has to face: identity, consciousness, and suffering

Every other approach is, in the end, an engineering question. AP11 is also a moral and philosophical one, and this is its most distinctive crack — the equivalent of the special, path-specific worry each of the harder bets carries. Three problems, none with an agreed answer:

  • The copy problem — is the emulation you, or just a copy of you? Today's scanning destroys the original brain. So a person is scanned, dies, and a mind that remembers being them wakes up in a computer. Is that survival, or has the person simply died and been replaced by a very good imitation that only thinks it is them? Worse: if you can make one copy, you can make a thousand. If ten emulations of one person run at once, which — if any — is the real continuation of the original? There is no settled answer; philosophers split between those who say what matters is the continuing pattern of memories and personality (so a copy is you) and those who say a copy is always just a copy. This is not a side-issue for AP11 — it goes to the whole point of "uploading" a mind.
  • Is the emulation conscious — does it actually feel anything? If substrate independence (Leg 3) is right, the copy has inner experience — it can suffer, fear, hope. If that view is wrong, the copy is an empty puppet that acts like a person but feels nothing. We currently have no test to tell these apart, which leads straight to the darkest problem.
  • The suffering problem. An emulation is a research subject you can create, pause, copy, edit, and delete at will. If it is conscious, then a half-finished, buggy emulation could be a mind in confusion or pain that you made by accident — and every failed experiment might be the creation and destruction of a suffering being. A path whose experiments might be moral catastrophes carries a weight no other approach on this map does.

These are not reasons the bet is wrong — but they are reasons it may be, in part, forbidden, or at least far more limited than a pure engineering project. [Contested / open — these are unresolved ethical and philosophical questions, and they are decisive for whether the path is even permissible.]

The big question under all of these

Every doubt above folds into one question: can you truly get a mind by faithful copying alone — with no understanding of how minds work — and even if you can, is that a road worth or allowed to travel, given it likely arrives last, teaches us nothing, and forces questions about identity and suffering we cannot answer? AP11 says yes to the first part: the complete recipe for intelligence is already written inside every skull, and we can copy it out without ever decoding it. The doubters answer that a copy without understanding may be impossible in practice (the worm shows a wiring diagram is not a mind, Stuck #1), is beyond our powers for the foreseeable future (Stuck #2), would be overtaken long before it arrived (Stuck #3), and brings in moral problems no other bet must face (Stuck #4). And notice the clean contrast with its twin: AP6 asks how much of the brain do we need to understand? and answers "the algorithm." AP11 answers "none of it — just copy everything." It is the purest test on the whole map of a single question: is understanding necessary to build a mind, or can a good-enough copy do it blind? As of July 2026 this is wide open, and — unlike the mainstream bets — likely to stay open for a long time. [Contested — the key open question, and the longest-horizon one on the map.]

⚠️ Honesty box

  • "No theory needed" is its strength and its ceiling. AP11 alone needs no possibly-wrong theory of intelligence (Leg 1) — but that means, even in success, it produces no theory of intelligence either (Stuck #3). It could hand you a working mind you understand no better than before. Keep both halves of that in view. [Established.]
  • The connectome is the beginning, not the answer. The mapped fly and the mapped specks are real and exciting (Leg 2) — but a wiring diagram is not a running mind (Stuck #1). The forty-years-mapped, still-not-simulated worm is the standing proof. Do not mistake a connectome for an emulation. [Established.]
  • Scale separation is an assumption, not a fact. The entire bet rests on there being a level of detail that is "enough" to copy (Part 1). We do not know that level, or even for sure that it exists. If the essential detail runs very deep, the job is far harder — perhaps impossible — than the hopeful case. [Contested.]
  • This is the longest-horizon bet on the map. Every other approach is arguing about the next few years. AP11 is arguing about the next several decades at least (Stuck #2, #3). It is worth understanding as an idea — the pure "copy without understanding" idea — far more than as a near-term plan. [Established.]
  • The ethics are not a footnote. Identity, consciousness, and the risk of creating suffering minds (Stuck #4) are central to whether this path can be walked at all. A technically successful emulation could still be a moral disaster. [Contested / open.]
  • Numbers and names age. The 2024 fly connectome, the 2025 mouse and human specks, expansion microscopy's resolution, the Blue Brain Project's 2024 close, the compute and storage estimates — these are dated snapshots (2024–2026). The lasting parts are the copy-without-understanding idea, the scan→translate→simulate plan, the connectome vs running-brain gap, the scale-separation assumption, substrate independence, and the identity/consciousness problems. The specific milestones will move; the shape of the bet will not. [Established core, dated specifics.]

How to use this (if you want to direct AI work)

  • Use AP11 as the map's clean test of one question: is understanding necessary? When someone claims a route to AGI, ask where it sits between AP11 (copy, understand nothing) and the theory-driven bets (understand, then build). AP11 is the pure extreme of "no understanding needed." Placing a proposal on that line tells you what it is really betting.
  • Never accept a connectome as proof of a mind. If a project claims to have "simulated a brain," ask the Stuck #1 questions: do they have the dynamics (connection strengths, signs, chemistry, live state) or only the wiring? A map of connections is a real achievement and not a working mind. The worm is your reference: full map, four decades, still no living simulation.
  • Discount timelines hard. AP11 is the slowest path on the map (Stuck #3). Treat any near-term "mind uploading" claim as a warning sign; the honest horizon is decades, held back by scanning and compute powers we do not have. Budget your attention accordingly — this is an idea to understand, not a program to join this year.
  • Keep the scale-separation question in front of you. For any brain-copying claim, the decisive question is how much detail do you actually need? The whole feasibility swings on it, and no one knows the answer. A plan that waves this away is not being honest about its hardest unknown.
  • Take the ethics seriously before, not after. If emulations are ever built, the identity and suffering questions (Stuck #4) are not just abstract debate — they decide whether the work is permissible at all. Anyone directing such work must weigh the chance of creating a conscious mind that can suffer first.
  • What you hand to others: the scanning, the image-tracing, the simulation engineering. What you keep for yourself: judging whether a "brain simulation" is really a mind or just a map, holding the scale-separation unknown, weighing the decades-long timeline honestly, and refusing to let the deep identity/consciousness questions be treated as an afterthought.

Connections

  • Keep only three things: ① AP11 = copy a brain without understanding it. Scan a real brain completely (every cell, every connection = its connectome), rebuild that exact structure in a computer, and run it; a mind emerges because the copy is faithful — no theory of intelligence required (Sandberg & Bostrom, 2008). The plan is three steps: scan → translate → simulate. ② It is the opposite twin of AP6: AP6 extracts and rebuilds the brain's algorithm (understand the method, drop the biology); AP11 copies the biology and understands nothing — the purest test of whether understanding is needed to build a mind. Its first steps are real, accelerating science (the fly connectome 2024; specks of mouse and human brain 2025). ③ It is stuck on four steep cracks: a wiring diagram is not a running brain (the 302-neuron worm, mapped 40 years, still not simulated — a connectome is "wires with the components rubbed out"), the scale is beyond us (a whole human brain is a million-plus times the largest speck mapped; the flagship Blue Brain / Human Brain Projects under-delivered), even success is slow and teaches nothing (it likely arrives last and yields a mind we still cannot explain), and the ethics (is the copy you? is it conscious? could a failed run be a suffering mind?).
  • Down the ladder (already read): guessing the next word · scaling laws & the data wall — the loosely-brain-inspired, not-a-real-copy machine that AP11 is the far opposite of.
  • Its closest cousin and opposite twin: AP6 · brain-based — both start from "the brain is the only general intelligence we know," then split: AP6 understands the algorithm and rebuilds it; AP11 copies everything and understands nothing. Read them together to see the whole "how much understanding do we need?" axis.
  • Its other links: AP1 · scale — the mainstream bet whose speed is AP11's Stuck #3 (scale will very likely reach AGI first). AP10 · embodiment — shares computational functionalism / substrate independence, the idea that mind is the pattern, not the stuff (Leg 3), and a copied mind would also need a body or world to act in. AP5 · world models and AP6 — the brain AP11 copies is itself a prediction-and-planning machine.
  • How sure are we? That the brain is the one known general intelligence, that connectomics is real and accelerating, and that a connectome alone is far short of a running mind — [Established]. That copying-without-understanding could ever yield a whole-human emulation, that scale separation holds, that the copy would be conscious or "you" — [Contested, open, and long-horizon].

Check yourself (try one, from memory)

  1. Say the AP11 bet in one plain sentence, using the words copy, understand, and brain.
  2. Explain the watchmaker vs photocopier difference between AP6 and AP11. Which one needs to understand the brain's algorithm, and which needs to understand nothing?
  3. What are the three steps of whole-brain emulation? What does each one do?
  4. What is a connectome, and why is having the full connectome of the 302-neuron worm not enough to simulate it? (Use the "wires with the components rubbed out" picture.)
  5. What is the scale-separation assumption, and why does the whole bet depend on it?
  6. Give two of the four things that make AP11 stuck, and explain why the ethics crack (Stuck #4) is one no other approach on the map has to face.

The rest of the map is here. To see the machine AP11 is the pure opposite of — loosely brain-inspired, not a real copy — read guessing the next word.
